新发呼吸道传染病:快速识别和管理的临床方面。

Emerging respiratory infectious diseases: Clinical aspects for rapid identification and management.
作者： Thierry Rolling, Thomas Theo Brehm, Florian Krammer, Marylyn Addo, Stefan Schmiedel, Helmut Salzer
摘要中文翻译新兴呼吸道传染病越来越多地出现在人类与动物环境的交界处,并给临床医生快速发展的诊断、治疗和感染控制决策带来挑战。这篇叙述性综述总结了临床相关的病毒和细菌病原体,这些病原体可能表现为严重的呼吸道疾病,但在疾病早期很容易被误认为是常见的社区获得性感染。我们专注于人畜共患甲型流感病毒,包括当代 H5N1、中东呼吸综合征冠状病毒 (MERS-CoV)、汉坦病毒、尼帕病毒,以及炭疽、肺鼠疫、兔热病和类鼻疽等吸入性细菌威胁。这些感染可能从非特异性症状发展为严重肺炎、急性呼吸窘迫综合征、休克、神经系统并发症、播散性感染或死亡。及时识别取决于对旅行、职业和娱乐暴露、动物接触、暴发 信号和病原体特异性症状线索的仔细评估。多重分子诊断可以支持常见病原体的早期分类和排除,但靶向 PCR、培养、血清学、测序和参考实验室确认仍然至关重要。管理需要及时的支持护理、适当的隔离、公共卫生通知以及针对病原体的抗菌药物、抗病毒药物、抗毒素或可用的预防策略。通过整合临床表现、诊断、治疗、疫苗 和 One Health 考虑因素,本综述旨在将新发感染的知识转化为实际的床边准备。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该审查重点关注 禽流感 A 病毒、禽源人畜共患感染和合格的禽类亚型。它解决了这些病原体带来的临床挑战,特别是它们引起严重呼吸道疾病的能力,这些疾病在疾病早期可能被误认为是常见的社区获得性感染。核心问题是如何快速识别和管理这些新出现的呼吸道传染病。
证据基础与方法
这篇叙述性综述总结了临床相关的病毒和细菌病原体,这些病原体可能表现为严重的呼吸道疾病,但在疾病早期很容易被误认为是常见的社区获得性感染。该综述没有报告具体的数据库、检索日期或资格标准,但包括一系列用于识别这些病原体的诊断和临床方法。
共识与结论
禽流感 A 病毒(包括 H5N1)可从非特异性症状进展为严重肺炎、急性呼吸窘迫综合征、休克、神经系统并发症、播散性感染或死亡。这些病毒因其人畜共患起源和快速 进化 的潜力而受到特别关注。
争议与缺口
该审查强调需要仔细评估旅行、职业和娱乐暴露以及动物接触,以识别 禽流感 A 病毒。然而,它没有提供关于这些病毒的诊断方法或 治疗 策略有效性的具体证据。
科研与实践启示
禽流感 A 病毒的管理需要及时的支持护理、适当的隔离、公共卫生通知以及针对病原体的抗菌药物、抗病毒药物、抗毒素或可用的预防策略。整合临床表现、诊断、治疗、疫苗 和 One Health 考虑因素对于将知识转化为实际的床边准备至关重要。

Emerging respiratory infectious diseases: Clinical aspects for rapid identification and management.

Authors: Thierry Rolling, Thomas Theo Brehm, Florian Krammer, Marylyn Addo, Stefan Schmiedel, Helmut Salzer
Original abstract (English)
Emerging respiratory infectious diseases increasingly arise at the human-animal-environment interface and challenge clinicians with rapidly evolving diagnostic, therapeutic, and infection-control decisions. This narrative review summarizes clinically relevant viral and bacterial pathogens that may present as severe respiratory disease, but are easily mistaken for common community-acquired infections during early illness. We focus on zoonotic influenza A viruses, including contemporary H5N1, Middle East respiratory syndrome coronavirus (MERS-CoV), hantaviruses, Nipah virus, and inhalational bacterial threats such as anthrax, pneumonic plague, tularemia, and melioidosis. These infections may progress from non-specific symptoms to severe pneumonia, acute respiratory distress syndrome, shock, neurological complications, disseminated infection, or death. Timely recognition depends on careful assessment of travel, occupational and recreational exposures, animal contact, outbreak signals, and pathogen-specific syndromic clues. Multiplex molecular diagnostics can support early triage and exclusion of common pathogens, but targeted PCR, culture, serology, sequencing, and reference laboratory confirmation remain essential. Management requires prompt supportive care, appropriate isolation, public health notification, and pathogen-directed antimicrobial, antiviral, antitoxin, or prophylactic strategies where available. By integrating clinical presentation, diagnostics, treatment, vaccines, and One Health considerations, this review aims to translate knowledge on emerging infections into practical bedside preparedness.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on avian influenza A viruses, avian-origin zoonotic infections, and qualified avian subtypes. It addresses the clinical challenges posed by these pathogens, particularly their ability to cause severe respiratory disease that may be mistaken for common community-acquired infections during early illness. The central question is how to rapidly identify and manage these emerging respiratory infectious diseases.
Evidence base and method
This narrative review summarizes clinically relevant viral and bacterial pathogens that may present as severe respiratory disease but are easily mistaken for common community-acquired infections during early illness. The review does not report specific databases, search dates, or eligibility criteria, but it includes a range of diagnostic and clinical methods for identifying these pathogens.
Consensus and conclusions
Avian influenza A viruses, including H5N1, can progress from non-specific symptoms to severe pneumonia, acute respiratory distress syndrome, shock, neurological complications, disseminated infection, or death. These viruses are of particular concern due to their zoonotic origin and potential for rapid evolution.
Controversies and gaps
The review highlights the need for careful assessment of travel, occupational, and recreational exposures, as well as animal contact, to identify avian influenza A viruses. However, it does not provide specific evidence on the effectiveness of diagnostic methods or treatment strategies for these viruses.
Research and practice implications
Management of avian influenza A viruses requires prompt supportive care, appropriate isolation, public health notification, and pathogen-directed antimicrobial, antiviral, antitoxin, or prophylactic strategies where available. Integrating clinical presentation, diagnostics, treatment, vaccines, and One Health considerations is essential for translating knowledge into practical bedside preparedness.

作者： Andrew Omame, Congjie Shi, Dewan Md. Sumsuzzman, Woldegebriel Assefa Woldegerima, Jianhong Wu

Dynamical Systems Analysis of Avian Influenza Models: A Scoping Review of Mathematical Frameworks

Authors: Andrew Omame, Congjie Shi, Dewan Md. Sumsuzzman, Woldegebriel Assefa Woldegerima, Jianhong Wu
Original abstract (English)
Mathematical models have been widely used to investigate avian influenza transmission and disease dynamics. However, to our knowledge, no review has specifically and systematically examined avian influenza models from a qualitative dynamical systems perspective. Consequently, the modelling frameworks, analytical techniques, and theoretical findings generated from these studies have not been adequately synthesized. This scoping review will address this gap by identifying and classifying avian influenza models that employ qualitative dynamical systems analyses and synthesizing their key theoretical findings. The review will provide a clearer understanding of disease transmission, persistence, stability, and control, while identifying gaps and priorities for future modelling research

Consequently, the modelling frameworks, analytical techniques, and theoretical findings generated from these studies have not been adequately synthesized.

用于畜牧业动物疫情预测和决策支持的闭环人工智能代理:综述

Closed-loop artificial intelligence agents for animal epidemic prediction and decision support in livestock farming: a review
作者： Wang Y, Zhou Y, Li L, Xu H, Ni W, Li X
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述重点关注闭环人工智能代理在动物疫情预测和决策支持中的应用,特别是在畜牧业中。核心问题是确定哪些技术成分得到兽医或动物健康证据的支持,如何将它们整合到闭环决策框架中,以及哪些领域仍缺乏实证现场验证。
证据基础与方法
该综述是对 2015 年 1 月至 2026 年 6 月期间在 PubMed、Web of Science 和 Scopus 上发表的文献的叙述性综合。搜索关键词包括人工智能、机器学习、深度学习、强化学习、AI代理、兽医、动物疾病、畜牧业、流行病学监测、诊断、精准畜牧业等。该审查检查了技术组件及其与闭环系统的集成。
共识与结论
目前的证据支持人工智能兽医疾病 监测 所需的许多传感、诊断、表型和预测组件。这些包括分子诊断、生物传感器、成像、热监测、行为识别和时间序列预测。然而,连接感知、资源感知行动、结果评估和在线政策适应的完全集成的自主系统在很大程度上仍然具有前景。
争议与缺口
证据存在异质性,许多 监测 系统仍然支离破碎、集成度弱且难以一致评估。此外,现有的输入存在离散采样、图像分辨率不足、跨平台标准不一致等问题。该审查还强调需要对这些系统的农场级实用性、安全性、稳健性和成本效益进行前瞻性验证。
科研与实践启示
未来的研究应该集中在输入端的升级上,为AI智能体实现更早、更准确、更连续的状态输入。它还应该通过在不同场景下自动调整权重来解决时间、资源和多模式协作的挑战。最后,强调完善决策体系和负反馈调节机制。

Closed-loop artificial intelligence agents for animal epidemic prediction and decision support in livestock farming: a review

Authors: Wang Y, Zhou Y, Li L, Xu H, Ni W, Li X
Original abstract (English)
This review explores an emerging paradigm of AI agents for animal disease monitoring, emphasizing how such agents can transition from passive observation to active intervention through a closed-loop perception–decision–feedback architecture. The review then discusses how multi-modal inputs, time-series forecasting, resource-aware decision-making, and reinforcement learning can be integrated into an agent-based workflow. 2.1From diagnostic results to data streamsMolecular diagnostics have long been recognized for their ability to identify pathogens before the full manifestation of clinical symptoms, thereby opening earlier windows for intervention. Quantum-dot dual-channel immunochromatographic sensors developed for ASF and pseudorabies can produce results in approximately 20 min and demonstrate good agreement with commercial diagnostic kits, making them suitable for continuous population-level monitoring (16). These approaches often provide faster detection than traditional methods and exhibit strong analytical performance, although they remain constrained by background interference, sample stability in complex matrices, and scalability of production (19, 20). Animal diseases continuously threaten livestock production and public health, yet current surveillance approaches remain largely passive and fragmented. Conventional artificial intelligence models typically function as open-loop predictors, delivering static outputs that fail to accommodate the dynamic operational needs of veterinarians in real-world farm settings. We first classify multi-scale sensing technologies: at the micro-scale, automated molecular diagnostics and biosensors; at the macro-scale, computer-vision-based phenotyping. These technologies collectively provide the data streams required for continu...
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on the application of closed-loop artificial intelligence agents in animal epidemic prediction and decision support, particularly in livestock farming. The central question is to identify which technical components are supported by veterinary or animal-health evidence, how they can be integrated into a closed-loop decision framework, and where empirical field validation is still lacking.
Evidence base and method
The review is a narrative synthesis of literature published between January 2015 and June 2026 on PubMed, Web of Science, and Scopus. The search keywords included artificial intelligence, machine learning, deep learning, reinforcement learning, AI agents, veterinary, animal diseases, animal husbandry, epidemiological surveillance, diagnosis, and precision livestock farming. The review examines both technical components and their integration into closed-loop systems.
Consensus and conclusions
Current evidence supports many of the sensing, diagnostic, phenotypic, and predictive components required for AI-enabled veterinary disease surveillance. These include molecular diagnostics, biosensors, imaging, thermal monitoring, behavior recognition, and time-series forecasting. However, fully integrated autonomous systems that connect perception, resource-aware action, outcome evaluation, and online policy adaptation remain largely prospective.
Controversies and gaps
There is heterogeneity in the evidence, with many surveillance systems remaining fragmented, weakly integrated, and difficult to evaluate consistently. Additionally, existing inputs have problems such as discrete sampling, insufficient image resolution, and inconsistent cross-platform standards. The review also highlights the need for prospective validation of the farm-level utility, safety, robustness, and cost-effectiveness of these systems.
Research and practice implications
Future research should focus on upgrading the input end to enable earlier, more accurate, and more continuous state inputs for AI agents. It should also address the challenges of time, resources, and multi-modal collaboration by automatically adjusting weights in different scenarios. Finally, improving the decision-making system and negative feedback regulation mechanism is emphasized as a key priority.

A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

华盛顿州确认首次在奶牛中检测到 禽流感

Washington confirms first detection of avian influenza in dairy cattle
新闻简报华盛顿州农业部于 2026 年 10 月 7 日发布的新闻稿称,首次在奶牛中检测到 禽流感。然而,提取的证据仅包含广泛的导航和资源列表,不包含有关检测的任何具体细节,例如事件发生的日期、涉及的农场或畜群、受影响的动物数量或任何实验室确认。因此,无法确认有关病例数、死亡率、暴露风险或应对行动的具体信息。缺乏实质性内容意味着该报告的说法仍未得到证实,并且无法从所提供的材料中评估这种情况对公共卫生的影响。

事件
华盛顿州农业部宣布首次在奶牛中检测到 禽流感 病毒。确认状态:提供的证据中未报告;无法从所提供的文本中核实该公告。
规模、影响与风险
病例、死亡或暴露:所提供的证据中未报告。风险评估:所提供的证据中未报告。
应对、状态与不确定性
调查、测试或控制措施:所提供的证据中未报告。由于来源缺乏详细信息,不确定性仍然很高。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Washington confirms first detection of avian influenza in dairy cattle

News Brief
A news release dated 7 October 2026 from the Washington State Department of Agriculture claims the first detection of avian influenza in dairy cattle. However, the extracted evidence consists only of extensive navigation and resource listings and does not contain any specific details about the detection, such as the date of the event, the farm or herd involved, the number of animals affected, or any laboratory confirmation. Consequently, no concrete information on case counts, mortality, exposure risk, or response actions can be confirmed. The lack of substantive content means the report’s claims remain unverified, and the situation’s public‐health implications cannot be assessed from the supplied material.

Published: 2026-10-07  |  Publisher: World Health Organization (WHO)
A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

世界卫生组织突发卫生事件 EPI-WIN 网络研讨会:了解甲型流感 (H9N2):当前风险、“单一健康”行动和大流行防范 - 世界卫生组织 (WHO)

WHO Health Emergencies EPI-WIN webinar: Understanding Influenza A(H9N2): current risks, One Health action and pandemic preparedness - World Health Organization (WHO)
新闻简报世界卫生组织 (WHO) 于 2026 年 10 月 14 日举办了一次网络研讨会,题为“了解甲型流感 (H9N2):当前风险、统一健康行动和大流行防范”。该活动旨在解决 禽流感 A(H9N2) 带来的持续风险,该病毒继续在鸟类中广泛传播,并已引起零星人类感染。网络研讨会重点讨论了该病毒的流行病学状况、病毒学特征和全球风险评估。它强调需要在人类、动物和环境卫生部门采取协调一致的“同一个健康”行动,以加强大流行病的防范。讨论强调了世卫组织、粮农组织、WOAH 和环境署在 监测、禽流感 预防和控制中的作用。该活动是在世卫组织每年两次就流感 疫苗 成分进行磋商后不久举行的,强调了其与全球卫生战略的相关性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该网络研讨会定于 2026 年 10 月 14 日欧洲中部时间 12:30 – 13:45 举行。证据中没有明确说明事件日期,但发布日期为 2026 年 10 月 7 日。
地点与对象
该活动是由世界卫生组织(WHO)主办的虚拟会议。该会议涉及来自全球人类、动物和环境卫生部门的专家,重点关注鸟类中的 禽流感 A(H9N2) 及其散发的人类感染。
事件
世卫组织主办了题为“了解甲型H9N2流感:当前风险、统一健康行动和大流行防范”的网络研讨会,讨论与禽流感 A(H9N2)相关的风险和防范。
规模、影响与风险
禽流感 A(H9N2) 继续在鸟类中广泛传播,并引起零星人类感染,表明动物与人类接触面持续存在风险。该网络研讨会旨在提供有关 流行病学、病毒特征和全球风险评估的最新信息。
应对、状态与不确定性
此次网络研讨会是世卫组织促进协调一致的“同一个卫生”行动和大流行病防范工作的一部分。会议讨论了 监测、风险评估以及世卫组织、粮农组织、世界卫生组织和联合国环境规划署在预防和控制方面的作用。
A high priorityPublic-health news

WHO Health Emergencies EPI-WIN webinar: Understanding Influenza A(H9N2): current risks, One Health action and pandemic preparedness - World Health Organization (WHO)

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) hosted a webinar on 14 October 2026, titled 'Understanding Influenza A(H9N2): current risks, One Health action and pandemic preparedness.' The event aimed to address the ongoing risks posed by avian influenza A(H9N2), which continues to circulate widely in birds and has caused sporadic human infections. The webinar focused on the epidemiological situation, virological characteristics, and global risk assessment of the virus. It emphasized the need for coordinated One Health action across human, animal, and environmental health sectors to strengthen pandemic preparedness. The discussion highlighted the roles of WHO, FAO, WOAH, and UNEP in surveillance, prevention, and control of avian influenza. The event was held shortly after the WHO biannual consultation on influenza vaccine composition, underscoring its relevance to global health strategies.

Governing bodies World Health Assembly Executive Board Member States Portal Home/ Newsroom/ Events/ Detail/ WHO Health Emergencies EPI-WIN webinar: Understanding Influenza A(H9N2): current risks, One Health action and pandemic preparedness Chat GPT © Credits WHO Health Emergencies EPI-WIN webinar: Understanding Influenza A(H9N2): current risks, One Health action and pandemic preparedness 14 October 2026 12:30 – 13:45 CET Virtual meeting Reading time: 2 min (524 words) Wednesday, 14 October 2026, 12:30 –13:45 (CEST) Background Avian influenza A(H9N2) viruses continue to circulate widely in birds and have caused sporadic human infections, highlighting the ongoing risk of influenza viruses emerging at the animal-human interface. Addressing these risks requires timely risk assessment, coordinated One Health action, across human, animal and environmental health sectors, and sustained preparedness for pandemics through GISRS and its partners. Held shortly after the WHO biannual consultation on influenza vaccine composition, including the review of candidate vaccine viruses for pandemic preparedness, this webinar will provide the latest updates on A(H9N2) epidemiology, virus characterization, animal and environmental surveillance, and global risk assessment. By bringing together experts from human, animal, and environmental health sectors, the webinar will promote a shared understanding of current risks and strengthen coordinated preparedness for future zoonotic and pandemic influenza threats. Objectives The webinar will provide a One Health update and support awareness on avian influenza A(H9N2) and provides an opportunity for engagement around the emerging Strategic Framework to accelerate Quadripartite action on avian influenza. Specific objectives are to: Summarize the current epidemiological situation of avian influenza A(H9N2) at the human–animal interface, including recent human infections and the geographical scope and impact of its spread in bird populations. Describe the virological characteristics of circulating A(H9N2) viruses and those detected in sporadic human cases, including antigenic and genetic diversity, markers of mammalian adaptation, and implications for risk assessment, diagnostics and candidate vaccine virus development. Highlight the roles of WHO, FAO, WOAH and UNEP in surveillance, risk assessment, prevention and control of avian influenza, including wildlife, poultry production, ecosystem and public health considerations. Show how data and expertise from GISRS WHO Collaborating Centres and OFFLU contribute to global monitoring, virus characterization, risk assessment and preparedness for zoonotic and pandemic influenza threats. Antiviral Research: N70S/Y155H Combined Mutation of Neuraminidase of Avian Influenza H9N2 Virus confers oseltamivir and zanamivir resistance and pathogenicity to Mice - Avian Flu Diary
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 3 日发表在《抗病毒药物 Research》上的一项研究强调了在中国传播的 H9N2 禽流感 病毒的神经氨酸酶 (NA) 基因中出现了 N70S/Y155H 双 突变。这些 突变 在 1998 年至 2024 年间从家禽中分离出的病毒中发现,赋予对奥司他韦和扎那米韦的耐药性,降低了它们的有效性,特别是在严重感染时。体内实验表明,这些突变的H9N2病毒在未经事先适应的情况下对BALB/c小鼠表现出高致病性。该研究强调了与 H9N2 相关的人畜共患风险不断增加,因为它有可能与其他新型甲型流感病毒共享这些 突变。虽然低致病性 禽流感 (LPAI) H9N2 对公共卫生的威胁仍然中等,但研究结果强调了继续进行 监测 及其遗传 进化 和 抗病毒药物 易感性研究的重要性。CDC 指出,两个 H9N2 谱系(G1 和 Y280)具有适度的大流行潜力,并且已经开发了几种候选 疫苗。研究表明,这些 突变 可能会影响酶的构象或底物结合微环境,从而可能改变病毒的行为。作者呼吁更多地关注 H9N2 病毒的基因变化,因为它们可能会导致未来的人畜共患 传播 风险。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Antiviral Research: N70S/Y155H Combined Mutation of Neuraminidase of Avian Influenza H9N2 Virus confers oseltamivir and zanamivir resistance and pathogenicity to Mice - Avian Flu Diary

News Brief
A study published on October 3, 2026, in Antiviral Research highlights the emergence of N70S/Y155H dual mutations in the neuraminidase (NA) gene of H9N2 avian influenza viruses circulating in China. These mutations, found in viruses isolated from poultry between 1998 and 2024, confer resistance to oseltamivir and zanamivir, reducing their effectiveness, particularly in severe infections. In vivo experiments showed that these mutated H9N2 viruses exhibit high pathogenicity in BALB/c mice without prior adaptation. The study underscores the growing zoonotic risk associated with H9N2 due to its potential to share these mutations with other novel influenza A viruses. While the public health threat of low pathogenic avian influenza (LPAI) H9N2 remains moderate, the findings emphasize the importance of continued surveillance and research into its genetic evolution and antiviral susceptibility. The CDC has noted that two H9N2 lineages (G1 and Y280) have modest pandemic potential, and several candidate vaccines have been developed. The study suggests that these mutations may affect the enzyme's conformation or substrate-binding microenvironment, potentially altering the virus's behavior. The authors call for increased attention to the genetic changes in H9N2 viruses, as they may contribute to future zoonotic transmission risks.
Original source text (English)
Avian Flu Diary Covering Pandemic and Seasonal Flu, Emerging Infectious Diseases, public health, community & Individual preparedness. NOTE: All AFD blogs are written by a human - any mistakes are solely mine. Saturday, October 03, 2026 Antiviral Research: N70S/Y155H Combined Mutation of Neuraminidase of Avian Influenza H9N2 Virus confers oseltamivir and zanamivir resistance and pathogenicity to Mice Range Of Endemic H9N2 Viruses #19,357 LPAI H9N2 has gained a reputation for spreading uncontrollably in Asian and African poultry, for frequently reassorting with other viruses, and for increasingly spilling over into humans. Nature: Genetic diversity of H9N2 avian influenza viruses in poultry across China and implications for zoonotic transmission J. Inf.: Zoonotic Threat of Novel H6N2 Avian Influenza Virus with Internal Genes Exclusively Derived from H9N2, China, 2025 Emerg. Microbes & Inf.: Novel emerging reassortant H6 avian influenza viruses with internal genes from G57 genotype of H9N2 pose potential zoonotic risk WHO WPRO Reports 2 Recent H9N2 Cases From Chinese Mainland While H9N2 is rarely thought of as being in the same league as H5N1, the CDC lists two lineages (A(H9N2) G1 and A(H9N2) Y280) as having modest pandemic potential, and several candidate vaccines have been developed. As a standalone virus, H9N2 appears to pose a moderate threat, but it has frequently aided and abetted more formidable novel flu viruses (e.g. H5N1, H5N6, H7N9, H10Nx) by providing some or all of their internal genes (see PNAS: Evolution Of H9N2 And It’s Effect On The Genesis Of H7N9). Last month, we looked at report of 6 H6Nx reassortants - all carrying the internal genes from H9N2 - and 5 with its NA from H5N1, and 1 NA from H9N2. Of note, the H6N2 isolate showed dual receptor binding to both avian and human-like receptor cells. This promiscuous nature magnifies any changes that might increase LPAI H9N2's zoonotic threat, since they could potentially be `shared' with other novel influenza A viruses. Which brings us to a paper published this week reporting reduced in vitro susceptibility to oseltamivir and zanamivir among H9N2 viruses carrying dual N70S/Y155H neuraminidase mutations—a combination the authors suggest has become common among recently circulating viruses in China. The authors report as much as a 42-fold reduction in viral inhibition in one isolate tested, and a 23-fold reduction in another. Neither is enough to render oseltamivir or zanamivir useless, but it could reduce their effectiveness, particularly in cases of delayed treatment or severe infections. This is a fairly technical paper, so I've only posted a few excerpts. Those wishing a deeper dive will want to follow the link and read the paper in its entirety. I'll have a brief postscript after the break. N70S/Y155H Combined Mutation of Neuraminidase of Avian Influenza H9N2 Virus confers oseltamivir and zanamivir resistance and pathogenicity to Mice Shenyu Ma a 1, Yuting Chen a 1, Jianwen Zhu a 1, Yuhan Zong b c, Keyu Cai b c, Zhonglong Yang b c, Wenlei Wang a, Yunfei Guo b c, Yan Guo a, Xinyue Duan a, Ziyan Fang a, Nan Zhu a, Sujuan Chen b c, Pinghu Zhang a c 10.1016/j.antiviral.2026.106540 Highlights • H9N2 viruses recently circulating in China harbors novel N70S/Y155H dual mutations in N2; • H9N2 viruses with N70S/Y155H mutations exhibit high pathogenicity to BALB/c mice. • N70S/Y155H linked mutations in N2 confer resistance to oseltamivir and zanamivir, rather than peramivir. • Neuraminidase inhibitors exhibited limited efficacy on H9N2 virus with novel N70S /Y155H dual mutations. Abstract Recently, H9N2 avian influenza virus (AIV) poses a significant threat to both the poultry industry and public health. Therefore, investigating the impact of its neuraminidase mutations on susceptibility to neuraminidase inhibitors (NAIs) is of great importance for clinically preventing potential cross-species transmission of H9N2. In this study, our results revealed that NAI resistance-related mutations of most H9N2 viruses isolated from China during 1998 to 2024 mainly occurred at positions N70S, E119A/D/G/V, Q133K, Q136A, D151E, Y155H, I222V, R224K, E276D, R292K, N294S, and R371K. Among them, the combined N70S and Y155H mutations are common characteristics of H9N2 viruses circulating in recent years. In vivo experiments demonstrated that these H9N2 viruses harboring the combined N70S and Y155H mutations exhibit high pathogenicity to BALB/c mice without prior adaption. In vitro neuraminidase inhibition assays confirmed that these H9N2 viruses bearing the N70S/Y155H mutations exhibited greater reduced inhibition than their counterparts bearing the N70S or Y155H single mutation to oseltamivir and zanamivir, but remained highly sensitive to peramivir. Notably, the in vivo protective effect of NAIs against H9N2 virus with combined N70S and Y155H mutations is limited, possibly due to the restricted ability of these drugs to ameliorate the excessive inflammatory responses. Therefore, there is an urgency to strengthen research on epidemiological surveillance and prevention strategies for avian influenza H9N2 harboring these mutations. (SNIP) Discussion In this study, we performed phylogenetic and comparative sequence analyses of the neuraminidase (NA) genes from 125 H9N2 avian influenza viruses isolated from poultry between 1998 and 2024. Our results showed that early H9N2 viruses circulating in China mainly belonged to the G1-like lineage, whereas more recent strains were predominantly classified within the Y280-like lineage. Sequence analysis further revealed that most of H9N2 viruses harbored multiple mutations associated with reduced susceptibility to neuraminidase inhibitors (NAIs), including substitutions at catalytic or framework residues (E119A/D/G/V, R292K, N294S, and R371K), as well as N70S, D151E, Y155H, R224K, and E276D mutations, which may affect enzyme conformation or the substrate-binding microenvironment[25]. These findings suggest that H9N2 viruses may have gradually accumulated a genetic background associated with altered antiviral susceptibility during long-term evolution. (SNIP) Collectively, our findings provide new experimental evidence for understanding the relationship between the combined N70S and Y155H mutations in N2 and altered susceptibility to NAIs in avian influenza H9N2 viruses. This study expands current understanding of the susceptibility of H9N2 to NAIs and highlights the need for continued surveillance of emerging neuraminidase mutations of H9N2 viruses circulating in poultry. (Continue . . . ) While none of this necessarily elevates the public health threat of LPAI H9N2, this general trend of LPAI H9N2 to accrue resistance mutations bears watching, particularly when you consider the numerous other warnings we've seen of its zoonotic potential. 结构分析揭示 H5N1 禽流感血凝素如何稳定人类适应 - Bioengineer.org

Structural Analysis Reveals How H5N1 Bird Flu Hemagglutinin Could Stabilize for Human Adaptation - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报柏林自由大学和亥姆霍兹感染研究中心的研究人员于 2026 年 10 月 8 日发表的一项新计算研究提供了有关 H5N1 禽流感病毒如何适应人类 传播 的见解。该研究的重点是血凝素(HA)蛋白,它对于受体结合和膜融合至关重要。该研究以 2024 年的先前工作为基础,检验了 突变 在酸性条件下提高 HA 稳定性的能力,酸性条件是空气传播的关键因素。虽然该病毒已在全球传播,并已在美国奶牛中扎根,但尚未表现出有效的人传人 传播。研究结果表明,HA、聚合酶活性和受体结合特异性的多重协调变化可能是病毒适应人类所必需的。该研究为 基因组监测 提供了一个框架来检测这种适应的早期迹象,这可能标志着向大流行能力的潜在转变。然而,证据中没有报告确认的人类感染或大流行风险。

该研究发表于2026年10月8日。证据中没有明确说明事件日期,但研究重点是自2024年以来H5N1禽流感的持续传播。

该研究的重点是 H5N1 病毒的全球传播,特别是在美国,该病毒已在奶牛中扎根。该病毒主要影响鸟类,包括野生和家养鸟类,并已在哺乳动物中检测到。

《npj Viruses》上发表的一项计算研究分析了 H5N1 禽流感的血凝素 (HA) 蛋白的结构变化,以了解它如何稳定以适应人类的适应。该研究并没有产生新的实验数据,而是建立在 2024 年之前的研究基础上。

H5N1 病毒已在全球范围内传播,对鸟类种群造成了重大影响,并在美国的奶牛中建立了持续的哺乳动物间 传播 病毒。该研究强调了该病毒适应人类 宿主 的潜力,尽管这仍然是一个令人担忧的问题,而不是一个已证实的事件。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Structural Analysis Reveals How H5N1 Bird Flu Hemagglutinin Could Stabilize for Human Adaptation - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A new computational study published on October 8, 2026, by researchers at Freie Universität Berlin and the Helmholtz Centre for Infection Research offers insights into how the H5N1 bird flu virus could potentially adapt for human transmission. The research focuses on the hemagglutinin (HA) protein, which is crucial for receptor binding and membrane fusion. The study, based on prior work from 2024, examines mutations that increase HA stability under acidic conditions, a key factor for airborne spread. While the virus has been spreading globally and has established itself in dairy cattle in the United States, it has not yet demonstrated efficient human-to-human transmission. The findings suggest that multiple coordinated changes in HA, polymerase activity, and receptor-binding specificity may be required for the virus to adapt to humans. The study provides a framework for genomic surveillance to detect early signs of such adaptation, which could signal a potential shift toward pandemic capability. However, no confirmed human infections or pandemic risk are reported in the evidence.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Thursday, October 8, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Structural Analysis Reveals How H5N1 Bird Flu Hemagglutinin Could Stabilize for Human Adaptation by October 8, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Structural Analysis Reveals How H5N1 Bird Flu Hemagglutinin Could Stabilize for Human Adaptation Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram The highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) virus of clade 2.3.4.4b has been spreading across the globe at an unprecedented scale, devastating wild and domestic bird populations and, since 2024, establishing itself in dairy cattle in the United States with sustained mammal-to-mammal transmission. Although human infections have so far produced lower mortality than earlier H5N1 outbreaks, the virus’s expanding host range and its ability to replicate to high titres in bovine mammary glands have sharpened concerns about its pandemic potential. A new computational study published in npj Viruses now offers a detailed structural perspective on one of the key molecular barriers that still separate this bird virus from efficient human-to-human spread: the acid stability of its hemagglutinin, the trimeric spike protein that mediates both receptor binding and membrane fusion. Hemagglutinin, or HA, is the major surface glycoprotein of influenza A virus. Each of its roughly 500-amino-acid monomers is cleaved into two subunits: HA1, which forms the globular head that docks onto sialic acid receptors on host cells, and HA2, which forms the stem anchoring the trimer to the viral envelope. In highly pathogenic avian viruses, a polybasic cleavage site allows activation by the protease furin throughout the body, contributing to systemic infection in birds. After a virus particle is taken up by endocytosis, the acidification of the endosome triggers an irreversible conformational change in HA that exposes the hydrophobic fusion peptide at the N-terminus of HA2, enabling the viral and endosomal membranes to merge and release the segmented viral genome into the cell. The prefusion form of the HA trimer is metastable by design. It must be stable enough to survive the journey between hosts, yet primed to refold when the right pH is reached. If the trimer is too labile, it may fire prematurely in transmission droplets or early endosomes, destroying infectivity; if it is too stable, fusion may fail altogether. This balance differs markedly between host species. In wild aquatic birds, the natural reservoir of avian influenza viruses, greater acid stability helps virions persist in water. The jump to poultry is typically accompanied by acquisition of the polybasic cleavage site and reduced acid stability, while adaptation to mammals generally demands the opposite: a lower fusion-triggering pH, shifting from roughly 5.7 to 6.0 in avian strains to about 5.0 to 5.5 in human-adapted viruses. That extra stability helps the virus survive the mildly acidic environment of the human respiratory tract and of airborne droplets, a prerequisite for efficient airborne transmission. Discover more Learn Engineering Track Biotech Tour Colleges Donate to Research Find Conferences Clade 2.3.4.4b, which likely emerged around 2020 through reassortment between a highly pathogenic H5N8 strain and a low-pathogenic avian virus in Central Asia or Europe, still binds preferentially to avian-type α2,3-linked sialic acid receptors and shows only weak affinity for the human-type α2,6-linked receptors. Experimental work has shown that a small number of HA mutations, such as Gln226Leu, Asn224Lys and Gly228Ser in the receptor-binding pocket, can shift receptor specificity, and that increased acid stability is essential for airborne transmission in ferrets. Yet these receptor-shifting substitutions remain rare in circulating strains, suggesting that efficient human adaptation will require multiple coordinated changes across at least three viral functions: polymerase activity, receptor-binding specificity and HA stability. The new analysis, led by Daniel Christian Lauster, Christian Sieben, Matthias Ballauff and Andreas Herrmann of Freie Universität Berlin and the Helmholtz Centre for Infection Research, does not generate new experimental data. Instead, it builds on the deep mutational scanning study by Dadonaite and colleagues published in 2024, which used a lentivirus-based pseudovirus platform to measure how nearly every possible amino acid substitution in the HA of A/American Wigeon/South Carolina/2021, a clade 2.3.4.4b strain, affected receptor specificity, acid stability and antibody escape. The Berlin team took the stabilising mutations identified in that screen and asked a structural question: what, mechanically, do these substitutions actually do to the protein? Discover more Find Study Aids cancer Cancer Because no three-dimensional structure exists for the exact strain used in the mutational scan, the researchers used the crystal structure of the HA ectodomain from A/Texas/37/2024, the reference strain of the dairy cattle outbreaks, which shares 99.4 percent sequence identity with the scanned virus, differing at only three positions. Using PyMOL to map polar interactions and the Adaptive Poisson-Boltzmann Solver to compute electrostatic surface potentials at both neutral pH 7.4 and acidic pH 5.3, they dissected each stabilising substitution in molecular detail. The electrostatic calculations, performed at physiological salt concentrations, revealed a recurring theme: many stabilising mutations act by taming a patch of dense positive charge in the lower HA1 head domain that becomes more repulsive as acidic residues become protonated at low pH. In the HA1 subunit, the team found that substitutions such as Lys57Glu, Lys62Glu, Arg81Gly, Arg90Glu, Lys262Ile and Lys269Glu all reduce the strongly positive local surface potential of the lower head domain, diminishing electrostatic repulsion that would otherwise destabilise the prefusion trimer under acidic conditions. Other mutations work through different physics. Glu106Asp preserves the existing contact with Asn71 of HA2 while forging an additional interaction with Arg76 of a neighbouring monomer, thereby reinforcing the HA1-HA2 interface both within and between monomers. Thr318Ile, located near the fusion peptide pocket, enhances hydrophobic packing with surrounding residues, consistent with earlier work by Imai and colleagues showing that this substitution lowers the fusion pH by about 0.3 units. Histidine substitutions at positions 18 and 110 also stabilise the protein, likely because replacing these protonatable pH-sensing residues with non-charged amino acids removes a destabilising positive charge that appears at acidic pH. In the HA2 stem, where most mutations are destabilising because the region is so finely tuned, the researchers identified a distinct set of permissive sites concentrated in the long helix and its connecting loop. Here, hydrophobic substitutions dominate: Ile77Leu, Val91Met, Asp95Met and Leu99Met all introduce methionine or leucine side chains oriented toward the centre of the three-helix bundle, creating new networks of hydrophobic interactions between the long helices of the three HA2 subunits. Asp90Glu, by contrast, strengthens a salt bridge with Lys307 of the adjacent HA1 subunit, shortening it from 3.5 to 2.9 angstroms while preserving the surrounding polar network. Arg106Glu reduces repulsion between positively charged side chains projecting into the stem centre. The short helix, notably, tolerated no stabilising mutations at all, underscoring its critical role in maintaining the metastable prefusion architecture. The authors are careful to frame their conclusions as computationally derived hypotheses. The analysis treats each mutation in isolation and cannot capture epistasis, the phenomenon whereby combinations of mutations produce effects not predictable from their individual contributions. Recent work by Dosey and colleagues has shown that simultaneous mutations in the HA stem of clade 2.3.4.4b can stabilise the protein in ways not observed when the same mutations are studied one at a time. The study also deliberately excludes the wider viral context: neuraminidase activity must remain balanced with HA binding for efficient mucus penetration and viral release, and polymerase mutations in the circulating genotype B3.13 already point toward mammalian adaptation. Effects on acid stability, moreover, do not necessarily correlate with effects on cell entry, meaning the two phenotypes must be evaluated separately. Nevertheless, the structural framework has immediate practical value for pandemic preparedness. By identifying which positions in the circulating H5N1 hemagglutinin can accept acid-stabilising substitutions, and by explaining the mechanisms involved, the study gives genomic surveillance teams a prioritised watch list of mutations whose appearance in sequencing data would signal progressive adaptation toward mammalian hosts. The finding that HA retains substantial structural plasticity, with multiple independent routes to enhanced acid stability spanning electrostatic tuning, hydrogen-bond reinforcement and hydrophobic packing, is a sobering one: it suggests the virus does not need a single improbable mutation to clear this barrier, but could assemble the required stability incrementally. The authors emphasise that direct experimental validation with purified mutant proteins, biophysical stability measurements and mutant HA structures will be essential, and that HA-neuraminidase co-adaptation must be studied in parallel. As clade 2.3.4.4b continues its global expansion, this kind of structure-guided surveillance may prove to be one of the most sensitive early-warning tools available for detecting the molecular steps that could bring a bird virus closer to human pandemic capability. The study was published on October 8, 2026. The event date is not explicitly stated in the evidence, but the research focuses on the ongoing spread of H5N1 bird flu since 2024.
Location and population
The research is centered on the global spread of the H5N1 virus, particularly in the United States where it has established itself in dairy cattle. The virus primarily affects birds, including wild and domestic populations, and has been detected in mammals.
Event
A computational study published in npj Viruses analyzed the structural changes in the hemagglutinin (HA) protein of H5N1 bird flu to understand how it could stabilize for human adaptation. The study did not generate new experimental data but built on prior research from 2024.
Scale, impact and risk
The H5N1 virus has been spreading globally, causing significant impact on bird populations and establishing itself in dairy cattle in the United States with sustained mammal-to-mammal transmission. The study highlights the potential for the virus to adapt to human hosts, though this remains a concern rather than a confirmed event.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study provides a structural analysis of HA mutations that could enhance acid stability, which is essential for airborne transmission. News Brief
A recent analysis by the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign's Veterinary Diagnostic Laboratory has revealed that avian influenza has spread beyond a backyard coop on an Illinois farm, affecting both a cat and a rat. The study, published on October 6, 2026, highlights the potential for avian influenza to spill over into other animal species, raising concerns about zoonotic transmission. However, the report does not provide specific details on the number of cases, deaths, or the broader implications of this spread. The findings underscore the importance of monitoring avian influenza in both poultry and other animals to prevent further transmission risks. The study is part of ongoing efforts to understand the dynamics of avian influenza and its impact on public health and animal populations.

新闻简报H5N1 禽流感正在各种动物种群中迅速传播,包括养殖鸟类、奶牛、毛皮养殖场以及海狮、信天翁和南象海豹等野生物种。截至 2025 年 1 月,美国已有 1 人死于该病毒,而动物死亡人数惊人,阿根廷有超过 17,000 只南象海豹幼崽(占种群的 95%)死于该病毒,意大利一个毛皮养殖场就有 51,986 只水貂被杀死。科学家在美国奶牛身上发现了一种高致病性 H5N1 菌株(进化枝 2.3.4.4b),该菌株可能只需要一个 突变 就能够在人与人之间传播 传播。该病毒正在乳制品行业和其他动物部门传播,专家警告其有可能演变成更危险的形式。善待动物组织(PETA)和其他组织呼吁采取行动阻止疫情传播,强调人类活动在疫情中的作用,并倡导纯素生活方式以降低风险。目前情况仍在调查中,人们持续担心该病毒的 突变 和 传播 功能。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Is Anyone Safe from the H5N1 Bird Flu? - People for the Ethical Treatment of Animals (PETA)

News Brief
The H5N1 bird flu is spreading rapidly across various animal populations, including farmed birds, dairy cattle, fur farms, and wild species such as sea lions, albatross, and southern elephant seals. As of January 2025, one human has died from the virus in the United States, while the animal death toll is staggering, with over 17,000 southern elephant seal pups (95% of the population) succumbing to the virus in Argentina and 51,986 minks killed on a single Italian fur farm. Scientists have identified a highly pathogenic strain of H5N1 (clade 2.3.4.4b) in U.S. cows, which may only need a single mutation to become capable of human-to-human transmission. The virus is spreading through the dairy industry and other animal sectors, with experts warning of its potential to evolve into a more dangerous form. PETA and other organizations are calling for action to stop the spread, emphasizing the role of human activities in the epidemic and advocating for a vegan lifestyle to mitigate the risk. The situation remains under investigation, with ongoing concerns about the virus's mutation and transmission capabilities.
Original source text (English)
Is Anyone Safe from the H5N1 Bird Flu? Bird flu is a quickly evolving disease currently spreading like wildfire through the dairy industry, farmed birds, backyard chicken flocks, fur farms, zoos, and wild animal populations, with devastating consequences. The human death toll from avian influenza A(H5N1) sits at 464, with one human succumbing to the bird flu in the United States as of January 2025. The death toll for other animals is considerably more massive, causing global organizations to call H5N1 the “Animal Apocalypse.” A shocking 485 bird species and 48 mammal species have suffered fatalities. Wild animals—including bald eagles, condors, albatross, raccoons, skunks, badgers, minks, foxes, lynx, bobcats, tigers, leopards, otters, seals, sea lions, and dolphins—as well as domestic cats allowed to roam outdoors, have died en masse. Between 2022 and 2023, the death of roughly 30,000 sea lions was reported in South America, about 10,000 albatross carcasses were found in the UK, and over 17,000 southern elephant seal pups (95% of the population) succumbed to the virus in Argentina. Unsurprisingly, 51,986 minks were killed on a single Italian fur factory farm in 2023 after the virus spread through the entire operation in a matter of days. These are only four of countless tragedies. In addition to killing tens of thousands of animals used for their skins, tens of millions of chickens across the U.S. are being executed using slow and painful mass-extermination methods. During past outbreaks, farms have been caught killing animals via ventilation shutdown (VSD). In this cruel practice, ventilation fans are turned off to slowly suffocate the animals. “VSD Plus” incorporates heat, humidity, and/or carbon dioxide in the process—suffocating and baking them to death. As of early 2025, the human and animal death tolls are rising in the United States, with experts worrying that human-to-human transmission is possible. Scientists have already sounded the alarm that a highly pathogenic strain of the influenza H5N1 (clade 2.3.4.4b) virus has been found in cows in the U.S. and may only need to undergo a single mutation for it to be able to attach to human receptors. In other words, the H5N1 bird flu spreading among humans may be a single mutation away. As more outlets publish updates on this devastation, remember that human activities caused this epidemic—specifically, humans breeding, raising, milking, killing, and eating animals, and if we truly want to stop the spread of H5N1, we must live and promote a vegan lifestyle. What Is Bird Flu, and How Can You Avoid It? - People for the Ethical Treatment of Animals (PETA)
新闻简报本文讨论了 禽流感(即“禽流感”)的风险,强调其可能导致全球大流行并造成严重后果。专家警告说,病毒可以通过未煮熟的肉或蛋、受污染的表面或蛋壳传播。文章强调了工厂化农场的不卫生条件,过度拥挤和抗生素过度使用为病原体 突变 和传播创造了理想的环境。报告指出,中国使用金刚烷胺等抗病毒药物药物已导致耐药性,降低了其对人体的保护效力。虽然文章没有报告确诊病例或具体遏制措施,但它强调了解决这些情况以防止灾难性 暴发 的紧迫性。禽流感大流行的风险被描述为“非常高,有人说甚至是肯定的”,对全球经济和健康产生潜在影响。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

What Is Bird Flu, and How Can You Avoid It? - People for the Ethical Treatment of Animals (PETA)

News Brief
This article discusses the risks of avian influenza, or 'bird flu,' emphasizing its potential to cause a global pandemic with severe consequences. Experts warn that the virus can be transmitted through undercooked meat or eggs, contaminated surfaces, or eggshells. The article highlights the unsanitary conditions in factory farms, where overcrowding and antibiotic overuse create ideal environments for pathogen mutation and spread. It notes that the use of antiviral drugs like amantadine in China has led to drug resistance, reducing their effectiveness for human protection. While the article does not report confirmed cases or specific containment measures, it underscores the urgency of addressing these conditions to prevent a catastrophic outbreak. The risk of a bird flu pandemic is described as 'very high, some say even certain,' with potential economic and health impacts worldwide.
Original source text (English)
Experts have warned Congress that avian influenza, or “bird flu,” could kill one in every eight people worldwide—including 40 million Americans—and cause a collapse of the world economy. People who take chicken flesh and eggs into their homes may be putting themselves and their families at risk. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention and the World Health Organization, the virus can be caught simply by eating undercooked meat or eggs, by eating food prepared on the same cutting board as infected meat or eggs, or even by touching eggshells contaminated with the disease. Former U.S. Health and Human Services Secretary Michael Leavitt called the likelihood of an influenza pandemic “very high, some say even certain.” Just walk into any chicken or turkey shed on a factory farm and it’s easy to see why. One shed houses tens of thousands of birds who are never allowed outside. They are surrounded by their own waste and breathe ammonia-laden air that burns their lungs and damages their immune systems. The conditions in these sheds provide ideal breeding grounds for pathogens because birds live amid their own feces from birth to slaughter. Egg-laying hens are kept in stacked cages that become encrusted with waste. When one bird gets sick, the disease can quickly spread to all the birds in the shed. The giant corporations that run factory farms know that they have created ideal breeding grounds for deadly viruses and other diseases, so they dose the animals with massive amounts of drugs. In fact, in the U.S., chickens receive more than three times as many antibiotics (by weight) as humans do. But these drugs are only temporarily effective. Over time, pathogens can mutate and become immune to the medications, leaving humans defenseless against an outbreak. In fact, the widespread use of the antiviral drug amantadine to control viral outbreaks in animals on Chinese farms has made bird flu resistant to that drug, rendering it useless for protecting humans against a bird flu epidemic. The problem is not restricted to birds: Factory-farmed cows and pigs, who also live in crowded and unsanitary conditions, can get and spread influenza and similar viruses. Hans-Gerhard Wagner, a senior officer with the U.N.’s Food and Agriculture Organization, has called the “intensive industrial farming of livestock” an “opportunity for emerging disease.” Giant factory farms are veritable flu-making facilities. The risk posed by bird flu could not be more real or more dire. Senior U.N. System Coordinator for Avian and Human Influenza Dr. David Nabarro describes it as a threat to “the survival of the world as we know it.” Dr. Gregory A. Poland of the Mayo Clinic has called it “what could arguably be the most horrific disaster in modern history.” A contingent of scientists called together by the U.N.’s Food and Agriculture Organization and the World Organization for Animal Health agrees that bird flu is primarily spread by the poultry industry. Experts believe that it’s only a matter of time before a strain of bird flu causes a widespread human outbreak. These viruses are constantly changing, and weaker forms in birds are known to take just months to mutate into highly pathogenic forms for which no effective treatment or vaccine exists. Outbreaks of other strains are regularly detected—Pennsylvania alone detects 15 to 20 outbreaks each year. Factory farming and people who eat chicken flesh and eggs are putting the world at risk for a catastrophe. For more information about bird flu and how factory farming is largely responsible for the disease, read Dr. Michael Greger’s book Bird Flu: A Virus of Our Own Hatching
View five news elements
Time
Experts have warned Congress that avian influenza, or “bird flu,” could kill one in every eight people worldwide—including 40 million Americans—and cause a collapse of the world economy.
Location and population
Factory farms in the U.S. and China, with global implications.
Event
Experts warn that avian influenza (bird flu) could cause a pandemic with severe global consequences, including potential economic collapse and high mortality rates. The virus can be transmitted through undercooked meat or eggs, contaminated cutting boards, or eggshells. Factory farming conditions are described as ideal breeding grounds for pathogens due to overcrowding, unsanitary environments, and antibiotic overuse.
Scale, impact and risk
The risk of a bird flu pandemic is deemed 'very high, some say even certain' by Health and Human Services Secretary Michael Leavitt. The virus can mutate into highly pathogenic forms with no effective treatment or vaccine. Outbreaks of other strains are regularly detected, such as in Pennsylvania, which reports 15 to 20 outbreaks annually. The use of antiviral drugs like amantadine in China has led to drug resistance, making it ineffective for human protection.
Response, status and uncertainty
The article highlights concerns about the role of factory farming in spreading bird flu but does not provide specific details on current investigations, testing, or containment measures. It emphasizes the need for awareness and prevention, such as avoiding undercooked chicken and eggs, but does not report on any confirmed cases or official responses.

分析发现,禽流感 蔓延到伊利诺伊州农场的后院鸡舍之外

Avian influenza spread beyond a backyard coop on an Illinois farm, analysis finds
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 6 日发布的分析显示,2025 年 3 月伊利诺伊州农场的 高致病性禽流感 (HPAI) 病毒传播到家禽以外,感染家猫和野鼠。该病毒被鉴定为 H5N1,在所有 12 个猫组织和 15 个大鼠组织中的 11 个组织中均被发现,并且在大鼠尿液中也存在病毒 RNA。伊利诺伊大学厄巴纳-香槟分校兽医诊断实验室病毒学部门负责人王乐毅博士指出,该病毒具有 突变,使其更适合哺乳动物,这引起了人们对可能传播到其他物种的担忧。这项发表在《新发传染病》杂志上的研究强调了啮齿动物作为 野生鸟类、家禽和哺乳动物之间被忽视的联系的风险。敦促农民和兽医保持警惕,控制啮齿动物,并报告鸡群突然死亡的情况。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发 于 2025 年 3 月报告,分析于 2026 年 10 月 6 日发布。
地点与对象
该事件发生在距 储存宿主 湖两英里的伊利诺伊州农场,影响家禽、家猫和野鼠。
事件
分析发现,禽流感 病毒从受感染的家禽传播到家猫和野鼠,并在所有三个物种中都证实了该病毒。
规模、影响与风险
该病毒感染了 105 只家禽中的 100 只,其中 12 份猫组织和 15 份大鼠组织中的 11 份检测呈阳性。在老鼠的尿液中发现了病毒 RNA。适应哺乳动物的 突变 的存在引起了人们对可能传播到其他哺乳动物的担忧。
应对、状态与不确定性
实验室对所有三种动物来源的病毒 基因组 进行了测序,发现了几乎相同的序列,表明有共同的来源。该研究发表在《新发传染病》杂志上,研究结果被认为是基于证据得到证实的。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Avian influenza spread beyond a backyard coop on an Illinois farm, analysis finds

News Brief
An analysis published on October 6, 2026, revealed that an outbreak of highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) on an Illinois farm in March 2025 spread beyond the poultry to infect a domestic cat and wild rats. The virus, identified as H5N1, was found in all 12 cat tissues and 11 out of 15 rat tissues, with viral RNA also present in the rat’s urine. Dr. Leyi Wang, virology section head at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign Veterinary Diagnostic Laboratory, noted that the virus had a mutation making it more adapted to mammals, raising concerns about potential spread to other species. The study, published in the journal Emerging Infectious Diseases, highlights the risk of rodents acting as an overlooked link between wild birds, poultry, and mammals. Farmers and veterinarians are urged to be vigilant, control rodents, and report sudden die-offs in flocks.
Original source text (English)
Strategic Communications and Marketing News Bureau RSS Contact Search Research News Arts Business Education Health and Medicine Humanities Law and Policy Library Information Sciences Science and Technology Agriculture Earth and Environmental Sciences Engineering Life Sciences Physical Sciences Veterinary Medicine Social Sciences Campus News Announcements Campus Life Honors For Faculty and Staff For Journalists About Avian influenza spread beyond a backyard coop on an Illinois farm, analysis finds October 6, 2026 10:21 am by Liz Ahlberg Touchstone Biomedical Sciences Editor Health and Medicine Veterinary Medicine Share on Facebook Share on Bluesky Share on X Share via email Dr. Leyi Wang, the virology section head at the U. of I. Veterinary Diagnostic Laboratory, analyzed viruses from animal remains collected on an Illinois farm after avian influenza ravaged the farm’s flock. He found an adaptation allowed the virus to spill over from the flock to a cat and a rat. View Images CHAMPAIGN, Ill. — An outbreak of highly pathogenic avian influenza virus that nearly wiped out an Illinois farmer’s backyard flock also spread to a domestic cat and wild rats on the farm, an analysis of the animals by the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign Veterinary Diagnostic Laboratory and the University of Missouri Veterinary Medical Diagnostic Laboratory found. “Bird flu does not stop at birds. On this farm, a cat and a rat were infected too, most likely after contact with dead or dying infected birds, and any wild animal that scavenges or handles carcasses could be exposed the same way,” said Dr. Leyi Wang, the virology section head at the U. of I. Veterinary Diagnostic Laboratory and corresponding author of the report. After quickly losing 100 out of 105 of his backyard birds in March 2025, the farmer brought the remains of five birds and a rat to the University of Missouri. Located a mere two miles from a reservoir lake, the farm was ideally situated for avian influenza to travel from wild waterfowl to the flock. The Missouri lab collected a sample from one of the farm chickens, which unsurprisingly tested positive for avian influenza. Four days later, the farm’s primary veterinarian collected the remains of a cat and another rat and sent them to the U. of I. Veterinary Diagnostic Laboratory, as there had been a few reports of infections in cats at the time. Wang and his team examined the mammals and tested and sequenced the viruses collected. Both animals tested positive for the same subtype of H5N1 avian influenza as the chicken had. Wang’s team then tested tissue samples from assorted organs to see how extensive the infections were in the mammals; all 12 cat tissues were infected, as were 11 out of 15 rat tissues, and viral RNA was present in the rat’s urine as well. “The cat was expected, but the rat is the surprise,” said Wang, who also is a professor of veterinary clinical medicine at the U. of I. “Since rats live on nearly every farm despite biosecurity, they may be an overlooked link between wild birds, poultry and other mammals. Rodents live close to people, livestock and feed, so infected, shedding rodents could contaminate the farm environment and create new exposure routes.” The labs then sequenced the viral genomes from all three animal sources: chicken, cat and rat. They found that the sequences were nearly identical, indicating a shared source— likely spillover from the poultry to the cat and the rat, Wang said. Crucially, all three had a certain mutation that makes the avian virus more adapted to mammals, which raised a red flag for Wang. “Because the mammalian-adaptation marker was already present in the bird virus, crossing into mammals may not require new mutations,” Wang said. “Shared changes arising in two mammalian species are exactly the kind of adaptation signal worth watching.” The researchers reported their findings in the journal Emerging Infectious Diseases, a publication of the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. In light of the findings, particularly the mammalian adaptation and the presence of viral RNA in the rat’s urine, Wang cautions farmers, backyard coop owners and veterinarians to be vigilant. “For flock owners: keep poultry away from wild waterfowl, report sudden die-offs, control rodents and don’t let farm cats scavenge dead birds. For veterinarians: when poultry die of suspected HPAI, treat sick or dead mammals on the same premises as potentially infected and use appropriate precautions.” Read Next Humanities Book counters AI hype by examining how researchers talk about their work A new book by English professor John Gallagher aims to counter the hype surrounding artificial intelligence by focusing on AI researchers and their work. Health and Medicine Honors Life Sciences Lu receives NIH Director’s New Innovator Award Xiaotang Lu received the NIH Director’s New Innovator Award for her work mapping connections within the brain. Social Sciences Paper explores systemic racism as the default explanation for disparities Sociology’s treatment of institutional racism as the default explanation for racial disparities has stymied research and debate, a new study says. Time
The outbreak was reported in March 2025, with the analysis published on October 6, 2026.
Location and population
The event occurred on an Illinois farm located two miles from a reservoir lake, affecting poultry, a domestic cat, and wild rats.
Event
An analysis found that the avian influenza virus spread from the infected poultry to a domestic cat and wild rats, with the virus being confirmed in all three species.
Scale, impact and risk
The virus infected 100 out of 105 poultry, with 12 cat tissues and 11 out of 15 rat tissues testing positive. Viral RNA was found in the rat’s urine. The presence of a mammalian-adaptation mutation raised concerns about potential spread to other mammals.
Response, status and uncertainty
The labs sequenced the viral genomes from all three animal sources and found nearly identical sequences, indicating a shared source. The study was published in the journal Emerging Infectious Diseases, and the findings are considered confirmed based on the evidence.

动物福利组织批评犹他州水貂养殖场对禽流感 暴发 的反应 - KSL News

Animal welfare groups criticize response to bird flu outbreak at Utah mink farm - KSL News
新闻简报2026 年 8 月,犹他州农业和食品部确认盐湖县农场圈养水貂中出现 7 例 禽流感 病例。动物福利组织批评该州的应对措施“松懈得危险”,理由是缺乏透明度和生物安全措施。尽管 传播 和工人接触的细节仍不清楚,但受影响的水貂种群预计将失去约 80% 的成员。官员们不确定适当的遏制协议,因为这是美国第一个记录在案的水貂 禽流感 暴发。该州已发布口头保留而不是合法隔离,并正在考虑减少种群、隔离或 疫苗接种 作为选择。然而,疫苗接种 需要农业部长的批准,而且对水貂病毒的研究有限。对人类健康的风险被描述为“尚未被证明是高风险”,但对病毒 进化 和 突变 的担忧仍然存在。犹他州卫生部在调查仍在继续时获悉此案,突显出缺乏处理此类 暴发 的既定协议。
查看新闻五要素
时间
犹他州水貂养殖场的禽流感 暴发 于 2026 年 8 月报告,该州应对措施的详细信息于 2026 年 10 月 3 日公布。事件日期为 2026 年 8 月,发布日期为 2026 年 10 月 4 日。
地点与对象
暴发 事件发生在犹他州盐湖县的一个水貂养殖场。受影响的种群包括农场中的水貂(预计该农场约 80% 的种群将因该疾病而死亡)以及可能受到感染的工人。
事件
动物福利组织批评该州对犹他州一家水貂养殖场发生禽流感 暴发 的反应,称其“松懈得危险”。犹他州农业和食品部于 2026 年 8 月确认了 7 例圈养水貂 禽流感 病例,但有关 暴发 的详细信息仍不清楚。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 导致水貂中出现 7 例 禽流感 确诊病例,估计 死亡率 发病率为 80%。官员们不确定遏制该病毒的适当方案,也没有明确的证据表明水貂之间存在 传播 或工人接触。对人类健康的风险被描述为“尚未被证明是高风险”,但病毒 进化 和 突变 的潜力值得关注。
应对、状态与不确定性
犹他州农业和食品部正在对 暴发 进行调查,并已发出口头扣押,而不是法定检疫。他们正在考虑减少人口、隔离或 疫苗接种 作为选择,但 疫苗接种 需要农业部长的批准。据描述,这种应对措施缺乏先例和指导,对水貂病毒的研究有限。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Animal welfare groups criticize response to bird flu outbreak at Utah mink farm - KSL News

News Brief
In August 2026, the Utah Department of Agriculture and Food confirmed seven cases of avian influenza among captive mink at a Salt Lake County farm. Animal welfare groups have criticized the state's response as "dangerously lax," citing a lack of transparency and biosecurity measures. The affected mink population is expected to lose about 80% of its members to the disease, though details on transmission and worker exposure remain unclear. Officials are uncertain about the appropriate containment protocol, as this is the first documented avian influenza outbreak in mink in the U.S. The state has issued a verbal hold rather than a legal quarantine and is considering depopulation, quarantine, or vaccination as options. However, vaccination requires approval from the Secretary of Agriculture, and research on the virus in mink is limited. The risk to human health is described as "not yet proven to be a high risk," but concerns about viral evolution and mutation persist. The Utah Department of Health was informed about the case while investigations continued, highlighting the lack of established protocols for handling such outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
UtahFeaturesCountiesIdahoU.S.World Home Counties Police & Courts Business Politics Education Science Environment More Health Religion Traffic Voces de Utah Home Counties Police & Courts Business Politics Education Science Environment Health Religion Traffic Voces de Utah Advertise with us Report ad Animal welfare groups criticize response to bird flu outbreak at Utah mink farm Posted - Oct. 3, 2026 at 10:33 a.m. ADAlixel Cabrera, Utah News Dispatch After reports of a bird flu outbreak at a mink farm in Utah emerged, animal welfare advocates are describing the state's response to it as "dangerously lax." (Animal Wellness Action) 16     Save Story Leer en español Listen:4 Minutes 04:11 KEY TAKEAWAYS Animal welfare groups are criticizing Utah's response to a mink farm bird flu outbreak. Advocates demand transparency on mink-to-mink transmission and worker exposure findings. Officials face challenges due to lack of precedent for mink avian flu. SALT LAKE CITY — After reports of a bird flu outbreak at a mink farm in Utah emerged, animal welfare advocates are describing the state's response to it as "dangerously lax." Details about the outbreak are still publicly unclear. A spokesperson for the Utah Department of Agriculture and Food said that "due to the limited reach and ongoing nature of the outbreak, we are unable to answer some questions that are too specific about the case in order to protect the privacy of the producer." However, records from the Department of Agriculture show seven cases of avian influenza among captive mink in Salt Lake County collected and confirmed in August — though the data may be incomplete, the agency said on its website. Animal Wellness Action and the Center for a Humane Economy, two advocacy groups, criticized the lack of information released to the public, including the mink population of the affected farm, whether there was mink-to-mink transmission of the virus, its source, or any worker exposure findings. Advertise with us Report ad "This isn't an obscure infection in an isolated wild animal," Wayne Pacelle, president of Animal Wellness Action and the Center for a Humane Economy, said in a news release. "It occurred among intensively confined mink, animals with a documented history of amplifying respiratory viruses and providing opportunities for viral evolution. Americans deserve to know what happened and what government found." In 2020, COVID-19 killed about 12,000 mink in Utah, one of the top mink-producing states in the country. The bird flu outbreak is the first of its kind documented in the country, according to the Animal Welfare Institute. The group found through a public records request to the Utah Department of Agriculture and Food that the affected farm was expecting to lose about 80% of its mink to the disease, and that officials were uncertain on the appropriate protocol to contain the virus. Without a standard to quarantine the farm, an official said: "our only options at this point are to watch them die or get emergency vaccine approval." Leon Page, a Utah Department of Agriculture and Food public information officer, said that because this is the first avian influenza outbreak in mink in the country, there isn't an existing precedent for treatment or response. "However, (Utah Department of Agriculture and Food) staff also do everything they can during the process to support producers and make sure that we maximize safety and minimize damage wherever we can," he said. But animal rights groups were not reassured. "It only takes one unlucky mutation of this dangerous virus to trigger a human health crisis, and we can no longer ignore the threat posed by disease proliferation on mink farms," Kate Dylewsky, the assistant director of government affairs at the Animal Welfare Institute, said in a release. "The failure of state and federal officials to prepare for this scenario, or to implement important biosecurity measures in response to the outbreak, demonstrates how mink farming operates with almost no oversight, compounding the threat to the public." Advertise with us Report ad The agency informed the Utah Department of Health and Human Services of the case while it investigated the possibility of transmission to humans, "which has not yet proven to be a high risk," Page said. The department also issued a "verbal hold" to the facility rather than a legal quarantine, before officials continued their investigation. Similar cases in other countries were solved through depopulation instead of quarantines, "so there is also little international precedent for a protocol that doesn't immediately resort to depopulation," he said. The options the department has contemplated for the outbreak are depopulation, quarantine and vaccination, Page said. But the latter can only be approved by the Secretary of Agriculture Brooke Rollins, not by state departments. "Because (avian flu) in mink is new to the U.S., research is limited, and there is little guidance for dosage or efficacy in mink specifically," Page said. "If approved, the vaccine would be released to the state veterinarian and tracked closely by that office to ensure there was no diversion to poultry, dairy cattle, or other farms. The USDA is having internal discussions on when they can or would allow vaccinations." × The Key Takeaways for this article were generated with the assistance of large language models and reviewed by our editorial team. Time
The bird flu outbreak at the Utah mink farm was reported in August 2026, with details about the state's response emerging on October 3, 2026. The event date is August 2026, and the publication date is October 4, 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak occurred at a mink farm in Salt Lake County, Utah. The affected population includes the mink at the farm, which is expected to lose about 80% of its population to the disease, and potentially exposed workers.
Event
Animal welfare groups criticized the state's response to a bird flu outbreak at a mink farm in Utah, calling it "dangerously lax." The Utah Department of Agriculture and Food confirmed seven cases of avian influenza among captive mink in August 2026, but details about the outbreak remain unclear.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak resulted in seven confirmed cases of avian influenza among mink, with an estimated 80% mortality rate. Officials are uncertain about the appropriate protocol to contain the virus, and there is no clear evidence of mink-to-mink transmission or worker exposure. The risk to human health is described as "not yet proven to be a high risk," but the potential for viral evolution and mutation is highlighted as a concern.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Utah Department of Agriculture and Food is investigating the outbreak and has issued a verbal hold rather than a legal quarantine. They are considering depopulation, quarantine, or vaccination as options, but vaccination requires approval from the Secretary of Agriculture. The response is described as lacking precedent and guidance, with limited research on the virus in mink.

Fewer Than 1 in 10 Initial Screenings Asked About Sick Animals When Actors Posed as Bird Flu Patients - Medical Daily

Fewer Than 1 in 10 Initial Screenings Asked About Sick Animals When Actors Posed as Bird Flu Patients - Medical Daily
新闻简报最近于 2026 年 1 月至 6 月进行的一项研究显示,当演员冒充禽流感患者时,在急诊室、门诊诊所和紧急护理中心进行的初步筛查中,只有不到 10% 的人会询问有关与患病动物接触的问题。这些演习由纽约市卫生局和贝尔维尤地区新兴特殊病原体 治疗 中心组织,涉及 73 次模拟访问,演员代表 20 至 28 岁的青少年。该研究结果于 2026 年 10 月 1 日发表在 CDC 发病率和 死亡率 每周报告中,强调了 禽流感 A(H5) 感染早期检测方面的潜在差距。该研究强调需要改进筛查方案以防止病毒在医疗机构中传播。这些演习是未经宣布的,目的是测试设施是否能够识别可能的禽流感病例。结果表明,医护人员可能没有充分准备好识别和应对 禽流感 症状,引发人们对公共卫生风险的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2026年1月至6月进行,CDC报告于2026年10月1日发布。新闻报道于2026年10月3日发布。
地点与对象
演习在三个辖区的 69 个设施中进行:52 个位于纽约市,15 个位于纽约州其他地区,3 个位于新泽西州。演习涉及 73 次模拟访问,演员代表 20 至 28 岁的青少年。
事件
在 73 次未经事先通知的演习中,有 7 次当演员冒充禽流感患者时,医院工作人员询问了与患病动物的接触情况。进行演习的目的是测试设施识别 禽流感 A(H5) 感染并防止候诊室传播的能力。
规模、影响与风险
演习期间只有 9.6% 的初步筛查包括有关与患病动物接触的问题。该研究强调了 禽流感 早期检测的潜在差距,这可能导致医疗机构中 传播 的风险增加。
应对、状态与不确定性
这些演习是纽约市卫生局和贝尔维尤地区新兴特殊病原体 治疗 中心准备活动的一部分。研究结果基于模拟场景,没有涉及真实的患者。该研究是初步的,旨在改进筛查方案。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Fewer Than 1 in 10 Initial Screenings Asked About Sick Animals When Actors Posed as Bird Flu Patients - Medical Daily

News Brief
A recent study conducted from January through June 2026 revealed that fewer than 10% of initial screenings in emergency departments, outpatient clinics, and urgent care centers included questions about contact with sick animals when actors posed as bird flu patients. The drills, organized by the NYC Health Department and the Regional Emerging Special Pathogen Treatment Center at Bellevue, involved 73 simulated visits with actors representing 20- to 28-year-olds. The findings, published in the CDC Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report on October 1, 2026, highlight a potential gap in early detection of avian influenza A(H5) infections. The study underscores the need for improved screening protocols to prevent the spread of the virus in healthcare settings. The drills were unannounced and tested whether facilities could identify possible avian flu cases. The results suggest that healthcare staff may not be adequately prepared to recognize and respond to avian influenza symptoms, raising concerns about public health risks.

Healthy Living Fewer Than 1 in 10 Initial Screenings Asked About Sick Animals When Actors Posed as Bird Flu Patients Published Oct 03, 2026 11:00 AM EDT By Dorothy Brooks CN-STR | AFP via Getty Images Share on Twitter Share on Facebook Share on Pocket When trained actors walked into emergency departments, hospital outpatient clinics, and urgent care centers with pink eye, a reported fever, and a cough, staff doing the initial screening asked about contact with sick or dead birds, livestock, or other mammals in only 7 of 73 drills, or 9.6%. The finding comes from a CDC Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report published October 1, 2026. No real patients were involved. Every "patient" was a professional actor, and every visit was an unannounced drill testing whether facilities could recognize a possible avian influenza A(H5) infection and keep it from spreading in a waiting room. That matters to anyone who has sat in a crowded emergency room during flu season. Bird flu can look like an ordinary respiratory illness, and the question most likely to flag it (have you been around sick animals?) was usually skipped at the front desk. Inside the Unannounced Drills Across Three Jurisdictions The 73 drills took place at 69 facilities from January through June 2026. Of those drills, 52 were in New York City, 15 in the rest of New York State, three in New Jersey, and three in the U.S. Virgin Islands. Forty-two took place in emergency departments, 19 in hospital outpatient clinics, and 12 in urgent care centers. The NYC Health Department ran the drills with the Regional Emerging Special Pathogen Treatment Center at NYC Health + Hospitals/Bellevue, which announced the voluntary mystery patient program for avian flu preparedness in April. Actors from the NYU Standardized Patient Program carried out the visits. Each actor played a 20- to 28-year-old who developed pink eye, then fever, muscle aches, and a cough after trying to rescue a sick duck in Central Park without wearing gloves. Makeup, petroleum jelly, and eye drops mimicked conjunctivitis. When staff asked about sick contacts, the actor replied, "Does a sick bird count?" Each drill lasted no more than two hours and ended after the first clinical evaluation or a decision to notify the health department. Staff debriefings followed. Masks Came Quickly, but Isolation and Protective Gear Lagged Staff screened for symptoms in 93.2% of drills and asked about travel in 79.5%. The actor was given a mask in 82.2% of drills, at a median of two minutes after arrival, but only 43.1% of timed drills met the one-minute target. Isolation was weaker. The actor was placed in an isolation room in 71.2% of drills, at a median of 11 minutes, and fewer than half of those placements met the 10-minute goal. Only 23 of the 52 placements were negative-pressure airborne infection isolation rooms. Overall, 44 drills (60.3%) counted as successful, meaning the patient was both masked and isolated. Protective equipment showed the clearest gap. Among 68 clinician encounters, the clinician wore all required gear (gloves, mask, gown, and eye protection) in only 25% and wore none in 5.9%. Urgent care clinicians wore full gear in one of 12 drills. Staff identified the actor as at risk for avian influenza in 63.4% of drills, and the health department was or would have been notified in 49.3%. Security guards, receptionists, or registrars met the actor first in 69.9% of drills. The authors wrote that both clinical and nonclinical public-facing staff could benefit from "role-specific, competency-based" infection prevention and control training. Exposure Questions Can Change a Diagnosis H5 infection has no unique symptom. CDC guidance for clinicians evaluating possible H5N1 patients lists signs such as eye redness, fever, cough, sore throat, body aches, and shortness of breath, which can resemble other respiratory illnesses. Without asking about contact with birds, dairy cattle, or other animals, a clinician may not order the specialized testing that public health laboratories and CDC perform. The authors wrote that "exposure-specific questions unique to avian influenza were asked considerably less often than were questions about general symptoms or travel history." Clinicians did better in the exam room. Among the 61 drills in which clinicians asked about exposures, they asked about sick or dead birds, livestock, or other mammals in 60.7%, which still left many visits without the question. The report has limits. Not every hospital in the region took part, relatively few clinics and urgent care centers were tested, actors could not show every sign of infection, such as an actual fever, and some staff may have recognized the drill. The findings describe gaps in preparedness, not missed infections. The CDC situation summary counts 71 confirmed and probable human H5 cases in the U.S. since February 2024, as of its March 6, 2026, update, with dairy and poultry workers among those affected. CDC says "the current public health risk is low," though its page on H5N1 bird flu in people notes that people with close or prolonged contact with infected animals face greater risk. If you develop red eyes, fever, or a cough after handling sick birds or working with livestock, mention it at check-in, not only to the doctor. Trouble breathing, chest pain, or rapidly worsening symptoms call for prompt medical care. The report points facilities and health departments to an updated mystery patient drill toolkit for running similar drills. Read more H9N2, H5N1, and H5N2 Are All Being Monitored for Human Risk — Here Is the Honest State of Avian Flu in 2026 Bird Flu Claims First US Victim As Louisiana Man Dies in Hospital Man In Mexico Dies From Bird Flu; First Human Case Of H5N2 Virus: WHO Key Questions Answered Were any real bird flu patients involved? No. All 73 drills used trained actors simulating H5 symptoms. Where did the drills happen? At 69 emergency departments, hospital outpatient clinics, and urgent care centers in New York, New Jersey, and the U.S. Virgin Islands from January to June 2026. Did only 1 in 10 clinicians ask about birds? No. The 9.6% figure reflects initial screening by front-desk and triage staff. Clinicians who asked about exposures asked about sick birds, livestock, or other mammals in 60.7% of those drills. Why does animal exposure history matter? H5 symptoms can resemble common respiratory illnesses, so exposure history can prompt isolation, full protective gear, and specialized testing. How many Americans have had H5 bird flu? CDC reports 71 confirmed and probable cases since February 2024, as of its March 6, 2026, update. What should I do if I get sick after contact with birds or farm animals? Mention the exposure when you arrive, wear a mask, and talk with a clinician. Seek prompt care for severe symptoms. 过去30天内有343,050只禽类受到影响,其中包括明尼苏达州斯特恩斯县商业火鸡农场的34,400只禽类和印第安纳州杰伊县商业鸭肉设施的24,300只禽类。德克萨斯州还报告在奶牛群中检测到新的 H5N1 病毒。
应对、状态与不确定性
美国农业部和动植物卫生检疫局正在监测事态发展,尚未指定进一步的行动。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

USDA notes more H5N1 avian flu on poultry farms

News Brief
The U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA) reported six new detections of H5N1 avian flu on poultry farms, including major outbreaks in Indiana and Minnesota. In the past 30 days, 550,000 birds in 12 commercial flocks and nine backyard flocks have been affected. Texas also noted new H5N1 detections in a dairy cattle herd, marking the third avian flu detection in cattle in the state.
